Bind it in the capture phase, or on the overlay root, and it fires first and throws away a half-typed note by closing the overlay around it. `overlay-stacking.test.ts` goes red if either moves.
- **While a reka popup or dialog is open, the overlay stack leaves Escape to it** (`composables/useOverlayEscape.ts`). reka's dismissable layers (Select, Popover, DropdownMenu, the range calendar's Popover, Dialog) hear Escape on `window`, which a keydown reaches after `document`, so the stack's handler runs first. It therefore returns without stopping the event whenever an open element carries `data-dismissable-layer`; a stopped event never reaches the popup, and Escape closes the whole overlay around a half-picked date range. A stack overlay that is itself built on a reka dismissable layer (the image lightbox's `DialogContent`) marks that element `data-overlay-layer`, or the stack would yield Escape to the overlay forever. A dialog that is a layer of the stack beneath the top overlay is not such a layer: the top overlay keeps Escape when every open dismissable layer is the root of an entry beneath it.
- **A host prompt is a modal layer of the stack** (`ExtensionUiDialog.vue`, `useOverlayEscape(onClose, { modal: true })`). It is a reka `Dialog` marked `data-overlay-layer`, mounted only while a prompt is queued, and paints on `MODAL_Z_INDEX`. A plain element under an open reka modal gets no pointer events and loses focus to the modal's focus trap; a nested reka layer, such as a reka Select popper, takes both. An overlay opened while a modal is up is inserted below it in the stack, so Escape and the Tab trap stay with the prompt the user sees. While the stack yields Escape to an open reka layer beneath the prompt, reka dismisses only its highest layer, the prompt, so nothing may close the settings modal on a window-level Escape of its own.
- **The settings modal is a stack overlay in both documents** (`components/settings/SettingsModal.vue`, `useOverlayDialog`). In VS Code it registers in the chat page's overlay stack; on desktop the overlay page mounts the same component (`src/desktop/shell/overlay/settings/OverlaySettings.vue`), where it registers in that page's own stack. Its selects bind `usePopperZIndex`, a host prompt (`ExtensionUiDialog`) stays the modal layer above it, Escape clears a non-empty search before it closes, and `OverlayApp`'s own Escape leaves a settings request to the modal. Host-only sections reach the modal through its `host` prop (`HostSettings`), never a host sniff.
- **A selection inside a group moves as one transform-only indicator** (`composables/useSlidingIndicator.ts`, `components/SlidingIndicator.vue`). The composable measures the selected item against its positioned parent and re-measures on resize; the component draws the box from fixed rounded caps and a 1px middle scaled on one axis (X for pills and tabs, Y for the `ring` and `soft` variants in vertical lists), so options of different sizes animate without animating width or height and without stretching the corners. It places itself without animating whenever its group mounts, so an opening popup does not fly the indicator in. A second indicator implementation, or one that transitions `width`, breaks the motion rules.
- **A dock prompt's options take focus as they mount, unless focus is in an editable field, another dock prompt or an open overlay** (`useDockPromptOptionsFocus` in `composables/useDockPrompt.ts`, through `components/DockPromptOptions.vue`, the options list of the permission, question and skill cards). reka's `ListboxRoot` focuses its highlighted item one tick after it mounts, whatever holds focus; the composable holds that focus back with the root's `focusable` flag until the item is highlighted, so a card that appears while the user types in the composer never takes the keystrokes. This is the only focus-on-appear path: a second `focus()` call beside it would bypass the rule. The question card keys its list by question, so each question's options mount and follow the same rule.
- **A body control marked `data-overlay-initial-focus` takes focus on open, ahead of the frame's close button** (`useOverlayDialog.ts`; `OverlayShell`'s X is `data-overlay-fallback-focus`). A search box an overlay opens on carries the marker; marking the X instead makes every body marker lose to it.
- **Every full chat overlay is an `OverlayShell`; confirmations are the restyled `ui/alert-dialog`** (`components/OverlayShell.vue`, `components/ui/alert-dialog/`). The shell gives each overlay its stack registration, focus trap and return, and the reference header (icon tile, title, subtitle, chip, `OverlayHeaderAction` buttons); an overlay that builds its own frame or opens on `ui/dialog` loses Escape ordering. App.vue mounts each overlay with `v-if` inside a `t-overlay` `<Transition>`, so its exit plays. A yes/no question (delete a session, rewind, rewind before a compaction, overwrite a settings file, remove all voice files) stays an alert dialog: it is modal by role and has no header actions. The image lightbox and the host prompt stay on their reka dialogs, which mark `data-overlay-layer` and are already handled by the stack.
- **Binding a plan file takes an id core issued, never a path** (`requestPlanFileCandidates` and `bindPlanToSession` in `core/chat-panel/message-router/handlers/workspace-handlers.ts`, `components/BindPlanOverlay.vue`). Core lists the folder's plan files from `listWorkspaceFiles` (the `@` autocomplete's excludes apply): markdown under a `plans` folder, or whose name has "plan" as a word. It issues a random id per file and keeps the ids per panel with the session they were listed for, replacing them with every list and dropping them when the panel closes; a folder switch replaces the session, so it retires them. Core answers every request, or the overlay never leaves loading: a listing that throws answers `listFailed`, and one that outlives its session or panel answers an empty list and issues nothing. The webview store closes on a session or folder change. `bindPlanToSession { candidateId }` binds only a file behind an id from that panel's last list for its current session; any other id binds nothing and is logged. Without an id core opens the OS file dialog, whose answer never passes through the webview, so no webview message names a file for core to read. Choosing a file in the overlay is the overwrite confirmation: the session's existing plan file is overwritten in place.
- **Popper content inside an overlay paints one above it** (`usePopperZIndex` in `composables/useOverlayEscape.ts`, bound by `ui/popover/PopoverContent.vue`, `ui/select/SelectContent.vue`, `SettingSelect.vue` and the two `ui/dropdown-menu` contents; a dialog is not popper content but a layer of the stack, above). Popper content is portalled to `body`, so it stacks against the overlay rather than inside it; a fixed `z-50` ties with an overlay at the base of the stack and sinks behind one opened at depth 1 or deeper. Every stack overlay provides its z-index, and the popper binds that plus one; outside any overlay it keeps `z-50`.

## Streaming views

- **Every view that streams output follows it through `useStickToBottom`, and nothing else writes its scroll position to the bottom** (`composables/useStickToBottom.ts`). The views are the transcript (`App.vue`), an `OverlayShell` given a `followKey` (subagent, team agent and side-question overlays), the thinking block (`ThinkingIndicator.vue`) and live shell output (`LiveOutputPane.vue`). Two desktop views keep the same rule through their own scroll APIs: a D36 log tab in Monaco (`CodeEditor.vue`) and the terminal in xterm (`TerminalView.vue`), which follows output only while `viewportY >= baseY` and whose Scroll to the end button calls `scrollToBottom()`. Any upward scroll stops following, and reaching the end again resumes it.
- **The view's position is the only evidence that the reader left the bottom.** Each read compares `scrollTop` with the last recorded value and the max scroll (`scrollHeight - clientHeight`) with the last recorded max. Following stops when the view moved up and either it is no longer within 2px of the bottom, or a user gesture holds it and the max scroll did not decrease. Outside a gesture, then, only a move out of the bottom band stops it: a script such as the prompt navigator's jump does, a clamp or rounding inside the band does not. During a gesture any upward move stops it, even 1px, so a short trackpad scroll is not undone by the next chunk. The position covers every way of scrolling (keys with modifiers, a scrollbar press, a wheel the browser latches to a nested scroller that has reached its end), which classifying the input cannot. A gesture a nested scroller takes (the thinking block inside the transcript, live output with `overscroll-behavior: contain`) leaves the view unmoved, so it keeps following.
- **A user gesture holds pinning; it does not decide.** Three inputs start a hold: `wheel` on the view (not a Ctrl zoom); `keydown` on the view's document, because keyboard scrolling can move the view while focus is outside it, for ArrowUp/Down, PageUp/Down, Home/End and Space, with or without Ctrl, Meta or Shift, but not with Alt, not once handled (`defaultPrevented`), not in text entry, and not Space on an activatable control; and `pointerdown` on the view, held until `pointerup` or `pointercancel` on the window, which covers mouse, pen and touch, scrollbar thumb and track presses, a text-selection drag and middle-click autoscroll. While held, a read still records the position and decides, but never writes `scrollTop`, because a pin written during a smooth scroll cancels its animation. The hold ends once the view has gone 150ms without scrolling: each wheel or key event and each `scroll` event while held restarts that window, and a pointer hold starts it on release, so momentum and track-click animations keep it alive. When it ends, a still-following view pins to the new bottom.
- **Following resumes when the reader is at the bottom: after a downward move, after the max scroll decreases (content shrinks or the view grows), on a downward gesture at the bottom where the view cannot move (a downward wheel; ArrowDown, PageDown, End or Space, also with Ctrl or Cmd), and through the jump-to-latest button.** `scrollToBottom` ends any hold and pins. The button calls `jumpToLatest`, which first focuses the view (`preventScroll`): the button unmounts once the view follows, and focus would otherwise fall to the body, outside a modal overlay. The transcript and a following `OverlayShell` body carry `tabindex="-1"`.
- **The transcript follows again on the user's own submit and when another session replaces it, and on nothing the host sends on its own.** `App.vue` calls `followTranscript()` in `handleSendMessage` and `handleQueueMessage` where the message is posted; the commands handled in the webview (`/rewind`, `/clear`, `/context`, `/usage`, `/stats`, `/btw`) return before it. `sessionCleared` returns `forceScrollToBottom`, because another session's transcript starts at its bottom. A host echo (`userMessage`, injected and command echoes included) and a background task's result do not move a reader who scrolled up. The status strip's Compact now posts `/compact` without following.
- **Growth is read from DOM mutations, and the pin is written in the same microtask.** The MutationObserver watches the view's subtree for child lists, text, and the `style`, `class`, `open` and `hidden` attributes. The transcript's canvas height changes inside its rows' ResizeObserver callbacks (`useScrollEngine.onItemResized`). A ResizeObserver on an ancestor is deferred to the next frame there, and the browser reports a ResizeObserver loop error, so a content observer would paint every streamed line one frame below the view. A ResizeObserver on the view itself covers the viewport changing size, and captured `load`, `animationend` and `transitionend` cover layout that changes with no mutation.
- **The transcript shifts the view for a row's resize only when the change can lie above what the reader sees** (`useScrollEngine.onItemResized`). A row wholly above the view shifts it by every change. A row the view starts inside shifts it only when it was measured before and was not streamed into. A streamed-into row is a row of the message still streaming, or one whose type or text changed since its last measurement: it grows at its end, below the reader, and its last growth can be measured after the stream ends (the last chunk, or the typewriter's unrevealed tail that renders at once when `streaming-text` becomes `text-block`). A first measurement only corrects the estimate of a row whose content is already on screen at its real height. Any other change of a row the view starts inside (an image loading, highlighting arriving) shifts the view by the change, so what the reader sees stays in place.
- **A reply's text keeps one row id from its first streamed character to its committed block** (`useVirtualizedMessages`). The trailing streaming text is `text-<message id>-<index of the block it is committed as>`, and a message with no blocks yet uses block 0. Measured heights are keyed by row id, so a new id at the end of the stream would make the finished reply a first measurement, whose estimate can be thousands of pixels off and, when it puts the row wholly above the view, shifts the reader by the error.
- **The compensation is written once per flush, after the canvas height commits and before the view's MutationObserver reads the position** (a `flush: 'post'` watcher on the frame). Written before the commit, a shift is clamped at the old bottom for a reader within the growth of it. It is an absolute target, the view's position at the flush's first compensated resize plus the sum of every compensated resize in that flush, because a shorter canvas has already clamped the view by the time the watcher runs. Running before the MutationObserver puts a following view's pin after the shift, so the pin lands on the new bottom.
- **A view starts at the bottom, except a finished thinking block.** An overlay starts again at the bottom when its `followKey` changes (another agent selected). The thinking block follows from mount only while it streams, so expanding a finished one starts at its top.

## Design tokens

- **One token layer colors every renderer, and only `tokens.css` reads a host theme variable** (`src/webview/styles/tokens.css`, `src/webview/__tests__/design-token-guard.test.ts`). The shared webview, the desktop shell page and the overlay read only `--d-*` tokens: surfaces (`bg`, `panel`, `card`, `hover`, `code`, `input`), `border` and `border2`, text (`text`, `muted`, `faint`), `accent`, `on-accent` and `accent-soft`, status (`success`, `warning`, `danger`, `info`), `on-danger` and `on-warning`, text on a tint (`accent-text`, `success-text`, `warning-text`, `danger-text`, `info-text`, `faint-text`), `add` and `del`, `shadow`, `scrim`, `font`, `font-size`, `mono` and `mono-size`. Beyond the reference's map, `font-size` carries VS Code's UI font size and `mono-size` its editor font size (desktop sets both to 13px), `on-danger` and `on-warning` are the text on a solid danger or warning fill, and the text-on-tint tokens keep tinted labels at AA (D48, below). The shadcn tokens in `style.css` derive from them, and so does every color the map lacks: the focus ring and primary fill are `accent` on `on-accent`, `accent` and `secondary` are `hover`, `destructive` is `danger` on `on-danger`, a link hover is `text`, blockquotes and table rows take `border2`, `code` and `hover`, and the chart hues are `accent`, the status colors and `color-mix` blends of them. The body text is `font-size`, and the root font size is `font-size` × 16/13, so 1rem is the reference's 16px at the 13px base and the whole rem scale follows the host's font size (`style.css`). The guard fails when any source under `src/webview` or `src/desktop` other than `tokens.css` names a `--vscode-*` variable. Core's two inline pages, the restore error page (`panel-manager.ts`) and the browser page (`core/browser/browser-panel.ts`), are host-neutral HTML outside the guard: VS Code pages load no `tokens.css`, so they carry the host theme style and read `--d-*` first with a `--vscode-*` fallback. The browser page's toolbar renders only in VS Code and keeps its VS Code variables.
- **Renderer sizes are rem, so they follow the host font** (`style.css`, `eslint.config.mjs`, `stylelint.config.mjs`). Text, line heights, spacing, radii and box sizes under `src/webview` and `src/desktop/shell` are rem (px ÷ 16 at the 13px default font, where 1rem is 16px), so nothing moves at the default and a larger VS Code font scales the whole panel. px stays only for border, ring and outline widths, blur and shadows. Container queries are rem too (`@max-[43rem]:`): Chromium resolves rem in a container condition against the root font size, but in a media query against the initial 16px, so there are no viewport media queries. A rule that depends on the panel width queries the `app` container, which `body` establishes so portalled content can query it too (`@min-[45rem]/app:`). The number in a `--text-*` or `--radius-*` name (`text-12.5`, `rounded-10`) is its px at the default font, and `cn()` (`lib/utils.ts`) lists both ramps for tailwind-merge. The composer and the cancel note grow with `field-sizing: content` up to a rem cap, never a height written from script. A size that script compares or draws follows the root font size too: the header folds at 45rem and 35rem of measured width (`ChatHeader.vue`), `SlidingIndicator`'s `radius` is px at the default font, scaled by the root, and every other px number an API takes (popper `side-offset`, the transcript's row gaps, the settings layout's 45rem fold, the stats chart height) comes from `remPx(rem)` (`composables/useRemPx.ts`). It is reactive: no event reports a computed-style change, so a ResizeObserver on a 1rem probe tells it the root size changed, and an open popper re-positions. Icons are sized by a `size-*` class (px ÷ 4), never a numeric `size`, which lucide writes as px `width` and `height` attributes; `LoadingSpinner` and the `icons/` set take the class too. The composer's action row wraps once its labels have folded, so it never overflows down to the narrowest panel, VS Code's 170px side bar (desktop's chat column is at least `COLUMN_MIN_PX`, 300px at the default font, less the 1rem grip gutter). Lint enforces the rule: `better-tailwindcss/no-restricted-classes` rejects a px arbitrary value in a class or variant, `enforce-canonical-classes` rewrites one with an exact scale step (Tailwind folds a `0.25rem` radius into `rounded-lg`, which is 6px here, so that rewrite is ignored), and stylelint rejects px on the sizing properties in `.css` files and `<style>` blocks. `no-restricted-classes` also rejects a viewport variant (`sm:` to `2xl:`, `min-[…]:`, `max-[…]:`, `[@media…]:`) and stylelint a width or height media feature. `vue/no-restricted-v-bind`, `vue/no-restricted-static-attribute` and `no-restricted-syntax` reject a `size` on any element but the components whose `size` is a variant name (`Button`, `Toggle`, `ToggleGroup`, `ToggleGroupItem`, `ToggleSwitch`, `SegmentedToggle`), a `size` in an `h()` call, and a numeric popper offset. Lint covers both trees, each resolving classes against its own entry point (`src/webview/style.css`, `src/desktop/shell/style.css`), and `scripts/__tests__/rem-lint-scope.test.ts` proves a px size fails in each. Desktop fixes `--d-font-size` at 13px (`theme.ts`), so 1rem is 16px there. Only geometry main applies to native views stays px: `setBounds` rectangles, window minimums and the traffic-light position. A size main stores from a pointer drag (the sidebar width, a section size) is px too, and its minimum comes from `remPx`.
- **VS Code maps the tokens to its active theme** (`tokens.css`, per the reference's `Damocles VS Code.dc.html`): `bg` editor background, `panel` side bar, `card` editor widget, `hover` list hover, `border` panel border, `border2` widget border, `text` foreground, `muted` description, `faint` line numbers, `accent` link, `on-accent` editor background, the status colors from the terminal green, warning, error and info foregrounds, `code` code block, `input` input, `add` and `del` the diff editor's inserted and removed backgrounds, and `font`, `mono` and `mono-size` the UI font and the editor font and size. A variable a theme may leave unset (list hover and widget border in high contrast themes, the diff backgrounds) has a fallback in `tokens.css`. `accent-soft`, `shadow` and `scrim` derive through `color-mix`, and each `*-text` is its color mixed 70% with `text`. `on-danger` is black or white, whichever contrasts more with `danger`: relative color syntax computes WCAG luminance in `srgb-linear` and switches at 0.179, where both reach 4.58:1, so a destructive button reaches AA in every theme (VS Code Light Modern's `errorForeground` takes black at 6.26:1, where the editor background gave 3.35:1). `on-warning` is picked the same way from `warning`, for text on a solid warning fill (Light Modern's `editorWarning.foreground` takes black at 6.74:1, where the editor background gave 3.12:1). A VS Code theme switch recolors the panel live, because every token resolves through `var()`.
- **Desktop fills the tokens with the Damocles palettes** (`src/desktop/main/theme.ts`). The host theme `<style>` loads after the stylesheet in the chat views, the shell page and the overlay, so it overrides `tokens.css`; desktop emits no `--vscode-*` variable. The values are the reference's `Damocles Desktop.dc.html` palettes; a color with alpha is written `#rrggbbaa`, because Monaco parses only hex. `THEME_BACKGROUND`, which the window and every view paint before their page does, is each palette's `--d-bg`. `--s-*` (`kw`, `str`, `num`, `com`, `fn`, `type`) and `--d-ansi-0` to `--d-ansi-15` are desktop-only: only Monaco (`components/editor/useMonaco.ts`), the desktop Shiki theme (`composables/damoclesShikiTheme.ts`) and the desktop pages read them, and `tokens.css` never defines them. `src/desktop/main/__tests__/theme-parity.test.ts` checks that every `--d-*` a renderer reads is defined in both palettes and in `tokens.css`, that both palettes define the same tokens with all six syntax and sixteen ANSI colors, and that no other module reads a desktop-only token.
- **Every desktop text color reaches WCAG 2.2 AA (4.5:1)** (`src/desktop/main/__tests__/palette-contrast.test.ts`, D21): the text, status and syntax tokens on `bg`, `panel`, `card` and `code`, `faint` on `hover` (any row may be hovered), `on-accent` on `accent`, `on-danger` on `danger`, `on-warning` on `warning`, the text-on-tint tokens below, and every ANSI color on `bg`, `panel` and `card` except the slot of the background's own polarity (black in dark, bright white in light), which is a fill. Reference values that failed were moved in OKLCH lightness only, keeping hue and chroma, to the first value that passes (worst surface ratio before and after): dark `--d-faint` `#6b7785` to `#7f8b99` (3.83 to 4.57, worst on `hover`) and `--s-com` `#5f6b78` to `#768390` (3.22 to 4.51); light `--d-faint` `#7d8a97` to `#5d6a76` (3.14 to 4.57, worst on `hover`), `--d-accent` `#0a7fd0` to `#0272bd` (3.76 to 4.50), `--d-success` `#138a5a` to `#037e51` (3.88 to 4.55), `--d-warning` `#a86a00` to `#9b6200` (3.95 to 4.51), `--d-danger` `#d43a40` to `#cd333a` (4.17 to 4.52), `--d-info` `#4b6bd6` to `#4867d2` (4.27 to 4.50), `--s-com` `#8a96a3` to `#646f7c` (2.68 to 4.55) and `--s-type` `#a16207` to `#9f6002` (4.38 to 4.50). The tints (`accent-soft`, `add`, `del`) keep the reference values. The reference has no ANSI palette; the sixteen colors per palette are drawn from its status and syntax hues.
- **Text on a tint takes its color's text shade, and the tint stays the reference's** (D48, `palette-contrast.test.ts`). A label in a status or accent color on a tint of that color (chips, badges, notes, a selected option, an agent's initial, diff pills and signs), and accent or status text on `hover` (inline code, path chips, a hovered text button, a desktop notification center row's status lead, `rowLead` in `overlay/notification-view.ts`), take `--d-<tone>-text`; faint text on a selected (`accent-soft`), hovered or diff (`add`, `del`) row takes `--d-faint-text`. Each is its color mixed toward `text` (`color-mix(in srgb)`) just far enough to reach 4.5:1 on a 22% tint of its color, the strongest tint text sits on, over `bg`, `panel`, `card`, `hover` and `code`; `faint-text`, `text` and `muted` reach it on `hover` and on `accent-soft`, `add` and `del` over those surfaces. The test composites each tint the way CSS does. Desktop values (worst ratio; the plain color on its 14% tint): dark `accent-text`, `success-text`, `warning-text` and `info-text` are their colors (4.96, 5.01, 5.30, 4.80), `danger-text` `#ef7d82` (4.56; 4.00), `faint-text` `#939faa` (4.53; faint on those rows 3.16); light `accent-text` `#065993` (4.55; 3.49), `success-text` `#066344` (4.53; 3.53), `warning-text` `#784f08` (4.51; 3.52), `danger-text` `#9a2b33` (4.58; 3.45), `info-text` `#3952a4` (4.53; 3.53), `faint-text` `#55616d` (4.56; 3.65); `on-warning` is `#03121c` dark (10.18) and `#ffffff` light (5.07). VS Code's 70% mix raises the contrast in dark, light and high contrast themes alike, but cannot reach AA where the theme's own color already fails on its background: on a 14% tint Dark Modern reaches 4.85 to 5.15 (`errorForeground` 4.15, 4.14 on the plain editor), Light Modern 5.05 to 5.61 (`editorWarning` 3.51 and `errorForeground` 3.72, 2.78 and 2.99 on white), and both high contrast themes at least 5.39. A chip whose tone is chosen at runtime (`agentStatusChip`, overlay status badges, MCP, Compass and context usage chips, agent initials, the desktop Projects list's activity and Untrusted badges) takes `.d-tone-<tone>` (`style.css`), which sets that text color and `--tone`, the color its tint mixes from (`var(--tone, currentColor)`), so the tint never derives from the darker text.
- **`damocles.desktop.theme` and `damocles.desktop.reduceMotion` apply live** (`followThemeSettings` in `theme.ts`, called in `index.ts` before the window exists). The theme (`system` by default, `dark` or `light`; anything else reads as `system`) sets `nativeTheme.themeSource`, which also drives `prefers-color-scheme` and the native menus, and `nativeTheme`'s `updated` event re-themes every view. Reduce motion travels on `PanelTheme.reducedMotion` and becomes the `data-reduced-motion` attribute of each renderer's root element: main writes it into the shell and overlay HTML, and the preloads apply it on load and on each change (`preload/apply-theme.ts`). `style.css` cuts every animation and transition to 0.001 ms and zeroes animation delays under that attribute and under `prefers-reduced-motion: reduce`.
- **Desktop renders in Geist and Geist Mono, with Greek in Inter, and no font file reaches the webview bundle or the VSIX** (`src/desktop/main/desktop-fonts.ts`). The table names each upright woff2 subset of `@fontsource-variable/geist` and `geist-mono` and Inter's `greek` and `greek-ext` subsets; `vite.shell.config.ts` copies those files and each package's OFL text into `dist/desktop-shell/fonts/` under the table's stable names, and `theme.ts` declares them in `@font-face` rules with `app://damocles/desktop-shell/fonts/...` URLs and the packages' `unicode-range` (`theme-parity.test.ts` compares each range with the package's own CSS). The rules travel in the host theme style, so the shell page, the overlay and every chat view load them under the `font-src app://damocles` they already allow. `--d-font` is `'Geist Variable', 'Inter Variable', system-ui, sans-serif`, so Greek, which Geist lacks, falls through to Inter; `--d-mono` is `'Geist Mono Variable', ui-monospace, Menlo, monospace`. Italics are synthesized. `vite.config.ts` and `chat-panel-csp.test.ts` are unchanged.
- **`HostCapabilities.damoclesTheme` picks the code highlighting** (VS Code false, desktop true). VS Code keeps `getShikiTheme()`, matched to the VS Code theme's name and kind. Desktop uses the `damocles` Shiki theme, whose colors are `var(--s-*)` and `var(--d-*)`, so a desktop theme switch recolors highlighted code without highlighting it again. Monaco defines its theme from the `--d-*` colors and the `--s-*` token rules, re-applies it whenever the host theme style or the body's theme class changes, and remeasures its fonts after a webfont finishes loading.
- **`src/webview/styles/motion.css` is the one motion source** (`motion-source.test.ts`): the reference keyframes `d-pop`, `d-up`, `d-fade`, `d-zoom`, `d-spin`, `d-pulse`, `d-blink`, `d-slide` and `nt-in` from the Desktop template, the Chat Panel ones (`o-zoom`, `o-fade`, `o-up`, `o-sweep`, `o-grow`, `o-bar`, `d-shimmer`), their exit counterparts, and the easing curves `--ease-out` and `--ease-spring` on `:root`, outside the `--d-` family so the token parity test stays meaningful. Keyframes animate only `opacity` and `transform` (the reference's background-position `d-shimmer` excepted): the reference's box-shadow `d-ring` is a scaling pseudo-element and its shimmer text is the transform pair `o-glint`/`o-glint-hold`. It also holds the shared enter and exit classes (`t-*` for Vue `<Transition>`, `d-popper`, `d-dialog`, `d-scrim` and `d-collapsible` for reka content; a collapsible's height snaps while its content fades). A component that its parent mounts with `v-if` cannot play its own exit, because Vue skips the leave of a `<Transition>` that unmounts with its parent, so the mount site wraps it (App.vue wraps every overlay in `t-overlay` and the dock prompts in `t-up`). Transcript rows animate only while `useLiveArrivals` marks them as arrived live, never on mount, because the virtualized list remounts rows as it scrolls. `webview/style.css` imports motion.css, and the shell page and the overlay load it through that stylesheet; no other source under `src/webview` or `src/desktop` defines any keyframe or uses a Tailwind animation utility: the built-in `animate-spin`, `animate-pulse`, `animate-ping` and `animate-bounce`, or the `animate-in`, `fade-*`, `zoom-*` and `slide-*` classes of the `tailwindcss-animate` plugin, which is not installed. A spinner uses `.d-spinning`. The reduced-motion rules (`prefers-reduced-motion` and `[data-reduced-motion]`) also zero `animation-delay`, so a staggered entrance never hides content.

## Session store

- **A replayed user message carries when it was sent** (`history-loader.ts`). `userReplay.timestamp` is pi's message `timestamp`, else the entry's ISO `timestamp`, for a prompt and a steer chip alike; the webview shows it, and only a live message takes the webview's clock.
- **A resumed or restored conversation continues on the model and thinking level its file recorded** (`session-store/recorded-selection.ts`, `PiSession.adoptRecordedSelection`). pi resumes on the branch's latest `model_change` or assistant reply and latest `thinking_level_change` (`sdk.js` `createAgentSession`), but Damocles passes the panel's model explicitly, so the session factory reads the opened file first: a recorded catalog model (`modelValueOfPiModel`, after `migrateLegacyModelValue`, so a retired recorded model continues on its `LEGACY_MODEL_MAP` successor with the recorded level, which pi clamps for it) that resolves and is signed in becomes the panel's, with its level as the effort and thinking switch (`ThinkingManager.restoreRecordedLevel`); otherwise the panel keeps its default. pi records the effective level, so Ultracode returns as Max, which runs the same. Permission mode and YOLO are never persisted or restored: a restored chat takes the settings defaults, so a bypass turned on for one conversation never silently comes back.
- **Nothing may append to a session file after it is deleted.** pi's `SessionManager._persist` sets `flushed = true` on its first write and from then on appends blindly, without re-checking that the file still exists. So a late write recreates the path holding only that one entry. Such a file has no `session` header, `SessionManager.open` rejects it forever, `listPiSessions` skips it, and because it is not in the list it cannot be deleted from the UI. Damocles cannot fix the writer, so it refuses to create the precondition, at three points: every holder detaches before the `rm`; a writer that resumes after an `await` re-checks liveness; and the reader caches negatives so one bad file is re-read once per change, not once per rebuild.
- **Detach is routed by session id, not by "is this my panel".** Two panels resuming the same file get the same session id (it comes from the header), and `registerSessionMutator` is a `Map`, so the second registration displaces the first. `deleteSession` therefore detaches the registered owner and `ctx.session`, deduped. The panel you clicked in may be the displaced one, or may only be pointing at the session as a not-yet-started resume/fork target. A panel mid-`start()` has no runtime yet, so detach awaits `startPromise` before resetting; without that, `reset()` bails, `whenReplaced()` resolves instantly, and `start()` goes on to open a manager on the path being removed.
- **`whenReplaced()` must report reality.** `runtime.newSession()` can throw, and returns `{ cancelled: true }` without tearing the old session down when a `session_before_switch` handler cancels. In both cases the old session is still installed and still writable. The promise rejects for both, and the logging `.catch` hangs off a separate handle so the rejection stays observable to callers while never becoming an unhandled rejection. `MessageRouter` catches handler throws, so a failed detach surfaces to the user and aborts the delete with no per-handler try/catch.
- **The session metadata cache is a pure cache, trusted only per file** (`session-store/session-meta-cache.ts`). Each session dir's list metadata and prompts persist in `~/.damocles/cache/session-meta/<encoded dir>.<app version>.json`, and an entry is used only while its file's size and mtime both still match, so a stale or foreign entry costs a re-read, never a wrong row. That is what makes last-writer-wins across windows safe, and why the file carries a schema constant plus the app version (`AppInfo.version`): a change to `sessionFileMeta` or anything it calls must bump the schema, or a same-version dev build serves the old fields. The version is in the file name so two apps on different versions sharing a folder (the extension and the desktop app update separately) each keep a valid file instead of rebuilding each other's on every read. A writer removes the other-version files for its own dir, and the unversioned `<encoded dir>.json` of older releases, once they have gone 30 days unwritten, checking once per dir per process. A live session's metadata comes from its in-memory manager with the leaf at the last entry, which is where `SessionManager.open` of the file puts it; reading the manager's own leaf would cache a rewound branch the file does not show yet. The stat and that snapshot run synchronously back to back, so pi cannot append between them. That snapshot assumes this window's manager is the file's only writer; two windows holding one conversation live already corrupt the file itself. A live session's updates stay in memory until the next scheduled write or dispose, so a turn does not rewrite the cache file after every tool call.
- **`SESSION_META_SCHEMA` (`session-store/session-meta-cache.ts`) is 3.** `StoredSession.model` (the branch's latest `model_change` or assistant reply, pi's resume rule) joined the cached rows, so a cache file of schema 2 is rebuilt rather than served. Any change to what `sessionFileMeta` returns bumps it; `session-meta-cache.test.ts` guards this bump.
- **A stored conversation has one writer across processes: its session lease** (`session-store/session-lease.ts`). The lease is a `proper-lockfile` lock at `~/.damocles/locks/sessions/<sessionId>.lock`, outside every session dir so pi's listing, the session watcher and the list cache never see it. It is stale after 20 s against a 2 s heartbeat, so a killed holder's lease is taken over once stale, never before. `claimStoredSession` takes it with `lockSync` inside its synchronous check-and-bind step, after the in-process panel scan, so a claim never waits on a lease: a held one refuses the claim and the user is told the conversation is open in another Damocles window, where an open path offers to take it over (below). On Windows an `EPERM` from a lock another process is removing counts as held, and any other lease failure refuses the claim with the reason, logged; a claim never throws into its handler. A resume and a panel restore read the lease (`sessionLeaseBlocker`, which takes nothing) before they switch the panel's folder, so a refused conversation leaves the panel's own conversation and folder alone; the claim inside the switch stays the race guard. A lease whose heartbeat is more than three beats behind belongs to a holder that died or hung: the user is told so, and a panel restore refused by it is retried once, when the lease turns stale, unless the panel holds a conversation by then. The lease is per process: panels of one process share it by holder count, and the one-panel rule inside a process stays the panel scan's job. `PiSession.syncSessionLeases` keeps the leases equal to what `holdsSession` reports, so every unbind path releases through it: a switch or reset (the rebind), a delete (`detachFromDeletedSession` resets), a folder switch and a panel close (dispose, after the runtime's teardown has appended its last entry), and host dispose, because `ChatPanelProvider.dispose` awaits every session's dispose (the extension's `deactivate` and desktop's `before-quit` both run it), including those of panels closed before it: `PanelManager` tracks every session disposal it starts until it settles, which is what covers desktop's window close on Windows and Linux, where the loaded chats close before `before-quit`. proper-lockfile also removes held locks on a normal process exit. A session with no file yet (a fresh conversation, a fork's branch) is leased when it is bound, on the path pi will write, since the lock never touches the session file itself. When the lock is taken from a holder (`onCompromised`: another process found it stale, or it was deleted), the holder replaces its SessionManager's `_persist` with a no-op in that same tick, because pi's manager has no close and a detach lands only after an await, then detaches as for a delete and tells the user. Loss is detected at the heartbeat, so if this process's event loop stalled past the stale window while another process took over, an append queued ahead of the heartbeat can still land once. A file write outside a live panel (a delete, a rename or tag of a conversation no panel here holds) runs under the lease too (`whileSessionLeased`), as its writer: it is refused when another process holds the session or another write of this process is running on it, and while it runs no new holder of this process joins the lease, so a claim from another panel is refused ("being deleted or changed"). A delete takes the lease before any detach, so neither another process nor another panel can claim the session between the detach and the `rm`. A session id reaches the lock path only after pi's own id rule (`isSafeAgentPathId`).
- **A lease holder hands a stored conversation over when another process asks** (`session-store/session-lease.ts`). A process can hold a lease with no window to show it: VS Code Remote-SSH keeps an extension host whose renderer left without a terminate message alive for its 3-hour reconnection grace, its `deactivate` never runs and its heartbeat goes on, and a clean reload races the old host's `deactivate` the same way. Refusing until the holder dies is not enough. Beside each lock, never inside it (proper-lockfile removes the lock dir with `rmdir`), the holder writes `<sessionId>.owner`, `{ v: 1, nonce, pid, hostname, panelToken, acquiredAt }`, through a temp file and a rename, right after `lockSync` and again whenever the panel token it names changes: a token that arrives with `ready` after the lease was taken, or a holder joining or leaving. A failed write is logged once and retried at the next poll, and never fails or releases the lease. `nonce` is fresh per acquisition. On a normal release the holder removes the record before the lock, and only while it still names its own nonce; `onCompromised` never touches it, because the record then belongs to the process that took the lock. A requester asks with `<sessionId>.release`, `{ v: 1, nonce, requestedAt, requesterPid }`, naming the nonce of the owner record it read, so a record a crashed holder left behind matches no live lease. Every process that holds a lease reads each lease's request every 2 s, the heartbeat cadence, from one unref'd interval that runs only while it holds a lease. It polls rather than watches, because proper-lockfile itself polls mtimes (watchers are unreliable on network and remote filesystems, where `~/.damocles` may live). A request counts only when its nonce is the lease's and its `requestedAt` lies within `SESSION_RELEASE_REQUEST_TTL_MS` (20 s) of now either way, which tolerates clock skew between hosts. The holder then removes it and calls `onSessionReleaseRequested` on each holder once, and asks nothing more for that lease until they all answered. A lease a writer holds (`whileSessionLeased`, or a handover's own writer) defers the request to a later poll. Every lease file is input from another process: a session id reaches a path only after `isSafeAgentPathId`, no path comes from file content, a read takes at most 1 KiB, every field is validated, and a missing or bad file reads as no record (a bad one logged once), never a throw.
- **A handover finishes this process's writes before the lease goes** (`PiSession.onSessionReleaseRequested`). It joins the lease as a `writer` first, synchronously: the detach's rebind releases the panel's own hold while the old session can still append, and while a writer holds the lease no panel of this process claims the session. Then it lets an in-flight start settle, stops a running turn through the Stop path (`interrupt`), so the `damocles-turn-stopped` entry lands while the lease is held, and detaches as a delete does (`detachSession`, which `detachFromDeletedSession` calls): the reset aborts the turn, drains checkpoint work for up to 2 s, installs a fresh session, posts `processing: false`, `sessionCleared` and the session state, and waits up to 10 s for the aborted agents. It tells the user "This conversation was opened in another Damocles window, so this panel closed it." and only then releases its writer, which removes the owner record and the lock. The writer's loss handler mutes the old manager, because after the reset the panel no longer holds the session and its own loss handler ignores it. A disposed panel ignores the request. A dispose that begins during a handover takes over: the handover stops, waits for the dispose's teardown, which appends the aborted turn, then releases its writer once. A replacement that fails leaves the old session installed and the panel's own hold keeps the lease, so the requester times out.
- **Only an open asks, and only the same panel asks without the user** (`chat-panel/session-ownership.ts`, the restore in `message-router/handlers/session-handlers.ts`). The webview makes a `crypto.randomUUID()` panel token once, keeps it in its persisted state, which every `setState` spreads and which VS Code's panel and view state and desktop's `panels.json` keep across a reload or restart, and sends it on every `ready`. No path copies one panel's state into another. The host keeps a well-formed token on the `HostInstance`, hands it to every session the panel runs (`setPanelToken`, on each rebind in `PanelManager` too, so a folder switch keeps it), and reads anything else as no identity. `requestSessionHandoff` writes the request, polls the lease every 250 ms, counting its own polls so a suspend never cuts the wait short, and returns `released` once the lease is free or `timeout` after `SESSION_RELEASE_REQUEST_TTL_MS`, always withdrawing its own request. The wait and the TTL are one constant: a requester withdraws its request when it stops waiting, so a request older than the wait was left by a requester that died, and the TTL keeps a holder from acting on it. A holder that picks up a live request near the end of the wait can still finish after the requester gave up. The conversation is then closed there and open nowhere until the user opens it. After `released` the caller claims through `claimStoredSession`, and a third process can still win that claim. A restore its lease refuses decides in this order: a holder whose heartbeat stopped keeps the retry at stale; a live holder whose owner record names this panel's token (this panel's previous host, orphaned or still deactivating) is asked in the background with no notice while the panel opens empty on its saved folder, and once it let go the conversation resumes (`resumeStoredSession`) unless the panel closed or holds a conversation by then; on timeout, and for any other live holder, the user gets the refusal with Open here. Only the open paths offer Open here (the restore, `resumeStoredSession` and desktop's unloaded-chat select, each passing a `LeaseTakeover` to `announceLeaseRefusal`), never a rename, tag or delete, and only when an owner record was readable. Choosing it re-reads the lease and the owner record, asks, and opens through the same open path. No toast choice and no takeover is awaited inside `ready`, the folder queue or desktop's chat queue. A holder from a release before handovers writes no owner record and reads no request, so it is refused with no Open here, and a stale record left beside its lock only times out into the refusal. Every refusal logs the holder's pid, hostname and when it took the lease.
- **A list upsert never applies metadata older than the list it lands on** (`chat-panel/storage-manager.ts`). A watcher event whose file still matches its cached size and mtime applies the cached row and never reads the file: on Windows a read updates the last-access time, which `fs.watch` reports as a change, so reading would re-trigger the event. An upsert captures the list generation before its metadata read and is dropped if the list was invalidated (rename, delete) or its folder closed meanwhile, because the list loaded since then read the file later. A delete event during an in-flight load invalidates that load, which may have listed the file before it went.
- **A prompt index has one definition, `pi-session/session-store/prompt-index.ts`, read by both the live session and the reload.** A prompt is a user message entry on the active branch other than one delivered mid-run (a queued batch or a cancel note, both keyed by a `damocles-mid-stream` entry), counted from the root. `PiSession.sendMessage` stamps a new prompt with `nextPromptIndex` of the branch it extends, `agent-start.ts` keys its injection with `nextPromptIndex` of the dispatch's own branch, and `history-loader.ts` stamps a replayed prompt as it walks the branch with the same `promptTest`, so the `userMessage`/`userReplay` index, the injection message's `details.promptIndex`, the Injected Context record, the permission turn key and the memory candidate name the same prompt live and after a reload. A per-panel counter cannot do that: a resumed or forked panel starts it at zero, and a reload that counts only the messages it displays restarts after every compaction. Counting from the root keeps an index unique within one session file, which is what lets the injection database key on it. That holds because Damocles never moves a leaf back within a file (a rewind forks to a new session and so a new database); anything that navigates a branch in place must first re-key those records by user entry id. A fork inherits its parent's indices below `nextPromptIndex` of the branch at the fork point, so `spawnPiFork` copies exactly those records into the fork's database through `MemoryService.copySessionInjections`; the fork's own prompts start at that index. A session-id migration whose source has no database leaves the destination's records in place (`renameInjectionDatabaseFile`), because every session start migrates from the panel id. The injection reads the dispatch's branch rather than panel state because pi commits the user entry only after `before_agent_start` and adds no prompt before it, while a run can start with no `sendMessage` in flight (a resent steer that finds the run settled). A cancel note's entry gets the mid-stream marker once pi commits it, so it consumes no index and reloads with the amber styling of its injected live echo. A run a note starts, because pi accepted it with no run in progress, dispatches before that entry exists, so `agent-start.ts` recognises it with `isCancelNoteDispatch` and keys it to `nextPromptIndex - 1`, the prompt the note annotates; it builds no memory injection, as a note steered into a running run gets none, because a record under that index would replace the annotated prompt's Injected Context, and it posts no injection chips. A note's run still sends a changed Compass status, keyed to that index; a note written before notes were marked has no marker, keeps the index it was counted with, and reloads as a prompt. An echo that commits no user entry (a pi extension command such as `/todos`, a locally handled `/compact` or `/init` notice) is emitted injected with the latest prompt's index, so it names no prompt and the next prompt keeps its own number. That uses the existing `isInjected` flag; never add a message type for it. A `/compact` echo is posted from `PiSession.compact`'s `onAccepted`, so one it refuses (a turn or another compaction running) leaves no echo, only its notice.
- **A reload shows a stopped turn as the live view did** (`session-store/turn-stopped.ts`). On Stop the adapter abandons each call in flight that pi has not started (no `tool_execution_start`, which pi emits for every call it handles, before the gate: `agent-loop.js:378-383`, `:414-419` in pi-agent-core 1.1.0), because pi sends no end for it, and drops any later assistant error, but pi still writes each started call's aborted result (its text varies by tool) and can write an error entry for the request the Stop cut off. Neither says the user stopped the turn, so `beginAbort` appends one `damocles-turn-stopped` entry after the run settles: every call in flight at Stop (`toolCallIds`), and the ids of the assistant error entries written after the leaf at Stop. A started call keeps its live card until pi ends it under the aborted signal, so it never shows Not executed first and then Stopped. A call pi executed was running when the Stop landed: pi kills it, records its error result with its `durationMs` (`:583-591`) and then emits its `tool_execution_end` (`:397`). The adapter shows that end, and `history-loader.ts` replays the call with that result and time; the stopped-result marker (see "Shell cancellation and process lifetime") makes both show it stopped, with its output. A call with no `durationMs` never ran (the abort settled it before `execute`, `:500-504`, `:519-523`, `:435-438`, a call waiting at the gate included), so the adapter abandons it at its end, and the loader replays it abandoned without the result pi wrote while stopping. An aborted run's `agent_settled` abandons any started call whose end never came, so no card spins after the turn. The loader drops the error rows the entry names. Never infer a stop from result text. A session written before the entry existed replays them as failed. Nested sessions (subagents and team agents) have no Stop path that writes the record, so the nested extension factory registers `registerAbortSettledCallRecord` (`stopped-tool-result.ts`), which appends one `damocles-turn-stopped` entry per call whose `tool_execution_end` has no `durationMs` while `ctx.signal` is aborted, whatever aborted the run. pi appends it and emits its `entry_appended` inside the extension pass, before listeners get that `tool_execution_end` (`agent-session.js:749`, `:2721-2726` in pi-coding-agent 1.1.0), so the live transcript reads the same entry a reload does: the subagent bridge sends `toolAbandoned` `stopped` instead of the failure and passes the ids to its sealing snapshot, and the team runner sends no `teamAgentToolResult` for the call and re-seals its message with it `stopped` (`resealStopped`). On reload `parseAgentEntries` collects the ids (`AgentFile.stoppedToolCallIds`), and `piMessagesToHistoryAgentMessages` and `memberHistoryMessages` mark such a call `stopped` with no result, by the main chat's rule: named and without `durationMs`. An agent file written before the entry existed replays the call as failed. Both the nested factory and the main extension register `registerWindDownErrorRecord`, which appends, as a `turn_end` draft, a `damocles-turn-stopped` entry whose `entryIds` names an assistant error entry whose `turn_end` arrives while `ctx.signal` is aborted (`isWindDownError`): pi dispatches `turn_end` from `finishTurn` once it persisted the message (`agent-session.js:524-526`, `:752-764`), so the entry id is known, and commits the draft before listeners get that `turn_end`, which is where every live transcript reads it (see "An error card is shown only for a model call pi will not re-run"). A Stop's own record leaves out an error that entry already names (`turnStoppedRecord`), so no error is named twice. `history-loader.ts` replays a named error with no card, its calls and the result-less calls of the batch before it `stopped`; `parseAgentEntries` maps the ids to their messages (`AgentFile.windDownMessages`), and `piMessagesToHistoryAgentMessages` and `memberHistoryMessages` replay each as an aborted stop with no error row. A file written before that record existed replays the error row.
- **A turn's `userMessageIdAssigned` names the entry its own `prompt()` committed** (`prompt-entry.ts`, read by the adapter through `promptEntryId`). The webview keys the prompt's rewind control, its fork point and its checkpoint marker on that id. Never read the branch's last user entry for it: pi persists a message only after its `message_end` has reached every listener (`_handleAgentEvent`), so at the prompt's own `message_start` the last user entry is the previous prompt's, and a steered batch or note can commit after the prompt within the same run.
- **Two writers append after an await, and both re-check liveness first.** `maybeGenerateTitle` waits on an unbounded sub-call (15 s timeout on the small/fast model); `persistTurnStopped` waits on `session.abort()` and appends only while the runtime session still has the stopped session's id and its lease was not lost, the rule `persistCheckpointRecord` uses. A delete or switch during a Stop calls `abort()` again, which waits for the same wind-down, so the record lands in the old file before the replacement installs. `recordMidStreamMarker` and `recordSteer` re-read `this.runtime?.session` and write synchronously; `renameActiveSession`/`setActiveSessionTag` re-read after `ensureStarted()`. `recordPromptSidecars` runs when pi commits a prompt's entry, which for a queued prompt can be long after `sendMessage` returned, so it writes only while the panel is undisposed and the runtime session is the one the prompt was sent on. Both sidecars sit under that one check, so a prompt gets both or neither.

## Checkpoints

- **A chat with no project folder takes no checkpoints.** The home target (`FolderTarget.projectScope` false: no folder open in VS Code, no project loaded on the desktop) runs in the user's home folder, and a baseline of it is a git snapshot of the whole profile: it never finished inside the wait limit, so every file-changing call stalled for the full wait and the turn was not rewindable anyway, while the shadow repo grew by gigabytes. `session-manager.ts` passes `projectScope` to `PiSession` explicitly, and with it false the panel creates no `CheckpointService`, registers none with the extension and hydrates nothing, and no gate context (panel, subagent, team agent) gets a `checkpointBaseline`, so nothing waits. A fork runs no checkpoint git work (no legacy clone, no record carry, no ref copy), so nothing creates or touches a shadow repo for the home folder. Only the conversation can be rewound: every prompt is a rewind point (`checkpointInfo` lists them live and `promptUserIdsOnBranch` on reload), the rewind view lists each prompt with `notRewindable: { reason: 'no-project' }`, which disables the file options, and runs no git (no row carries the Not rewindable badge in `RewindBrowser.vue`; the selected prompt's notes, `RewindCheckpointNotes.vue`, say why once), and a file rewind or undo is refused with a localized message. `ChatSession.fileCheckpoints` carries the rule to the history loader and the rewind view.
- **One shadow repo per folder, shared by every conversation in it** (`pi-session/checkpoints/`). The repo lives at `~/.damocles/pi/checkpoints/folders/<folderId>/`, where `folderId` is the first 16 hex of sha256 of `folderKey(cwd)` (the keying Compass uses). It holds one bare git dir, one index, `folder.json` (`{ path, layout: 1 }`) and the mkdir lock. The fixed-length id keeps `GIT_DIR` under git's `'$GIT_DIR' too big` limit for any project path. The shared index is the point: its stat cache lets a new conversation re-hash only the files that changed. Every git step that writes a folder repo runs under that repo's lock (`checkpoints/lock.ts`): an in-process queue per repo plus the cross-process mkdir lock with owner pid, heartbeat and stale takeover. The lock is not reentrant, so no code awaits a baseline while holding it, and only checkpoint git work (baseline, finalize, compaction snapshot, pre-rewind snapshot, restore, maintenance, ref delete and copy) ever holds it. No tool holds it. It never waits forever: a holder is stale once its heartbeat lapsed past 30 s and its pid is gone, or, whatever the pid says (a pid can be reused), once the waiter has itself polled for 10 minutes without seeing the heartbeat move. The bound counts the waiter's own polls, not wall-clock time, so a system suspend counts as one poll and never makes a live holder look stale. Every wait where the user waits takes an `AbortSignal` (`withRepoLock(dir, fn, { signal })` throws `LockWaitAbortedError`).
- **A git lock file a killed git left behind is removed only when no git of the folder repo can still hold it.** Every git command that can create `index.lock`, `packed-refs.lock`, `refs/**.lock`, `HEAD.lock`, `config.lock`, `shallow.lock` or `gc.pid` in a folder repo runs under the folder lock and registers its pid in `<repo>/git-procs/<pid>` while it runs. When a holder acquires the lock and any registered git still runs, nothing is removed. Otherwise the index, packed-refs, HEAD, config, shallow and gc.pid locks go, logged with their age, and `refs/**.lock` too when there is evidence of a dead holder: a takeover of the folder lock, or a registration whose process is gone, runs another program, or is over 24 hours old. A holder that released normally awaited all its git children, so a git lock left then is from a killed git. This assumes the spawned git process (on Windows the `git.exe` launcher, which waits for the real git) outlives the git work it started unless it is killed on its own.
- **Folder repo state is written atomically.** `size-excludes.json`, `info/exclude`, `info/attributes`, `folder.json` and `sessions/<id>.json` go through a synced temp file and a rename, the two `info/` files only when their content changes. An unreadable or malformed size state is logged and read as none, which costs a full rescan. A repo is built in `<repo>/.git-init-*` (init, identity, tuning, ignorecase, version stamp, attributes) and renamed to `.git` last, so `isInitialized` never accepts a half-built repo; the next init removes a leftover temp dir with a log line. The size state records every file over 1 MiB, the smallest cap the setting allows, so hosts with different `maxFileSizeMB` sharing a folder repo derive their size section without a rescan.
- **Snapshots and restores are byte-exact.** A folder repo's `$GIT_DIR/info/attributes` is `* -text -eol -ident -filter -working-tree-encoding`, written at creation and refreshed when it differs, so no line ending is converted, no `$Id$` expanded and no filter run, whatever the project's `.gitattributes` says. `diff` is left alone so numstat still counts lines. Legacy per-session repos do not get it: their blobs were captured through the project's attributes, so restoring through the same attributes is the closest to the original bytes. `core.filemode=false` is set only on Windows; elsewhere git's own probe applies, so an executable keeps 100755 and a restore brings its executable bit back. The rewind preview uses the checkpoint repo's exact config (`PORTABLE_CONFIG`: autocrlf and safecrlf off, filemode per platform, `core.excludesFile=` so no user-global ignore applies, `core.fsmonitor=false`, `core.hooksPath=/dev/null`).
- **Commits live only through per-conversation refs.** A snapshot is `write-tree` + `commit-tree` with no shared parent, and HEAD never moves, so nothing but a ref keeps a commit alive. A turn writes `refs/damocles/sessions/<sessionId>/<userEntryId>/before` and `/after`, plus `/skipped` for its skipped-file manifest; a compaction snapshot writes `.../<compactionEntryId>/snapshot`; a pre-rewind snapshot writes `.../_rewinds/<id>/snapshot` and `/skipped`. `_` cannot start an entry id, so that namespace never collides with an entry's refs. Ids go into ref names only after `isSafeRefId`. Deleting a conversation deletes its own ref namespace in every folder repo its entries name, under each repo's lock, after every holder detached and after its session file is gone, and nothing waits on it: that lock can be held by another conversation's first baseline for minutes. The folder ids are read from the file before the `rm`. Refs whose removal fails or never runs (the process exits) are left to maintenance's orphan rule below. A fork copies the refs of the checkpoints it inherits (and their manifests) into its own namespace and carries every checkpoint record whose `userEntryId` is on the inherited branch, wherever the record sits in the parent's file. The records are written before the fork opens; the ref copy runs after, without the user waiting on the folder lock, and checks each object under that lock when it runs, so a copy never names an object already gone. Before the fork opens, its registration in each folder repo those records name is written with `pendingCopy: true` (`markForkCopyPending`), without the folder lock, because nothing deletes a new session's registration; the copy clears the flag and keeps the file's mtime, which dates the fork for eviction. A fork's inherited checkpoints become its own refs before any ref deletion or prune in that folder repo: every lock hold that deletes a conversation's refs (delete, age eviction, orphan sweep) or prunes first runs `reconcilePendingCopiesLocked`, which copies each pending fork's refs from the validated v3 entries of its session file and clears the flag, or drops a pending registration whose session file is gone, so a copy the process exited before running is still made. The in-process lock queue is first come first served, so a restore or a delete of the fork queued later runs after the copy. A fork inherits no pre-rewind record or ref: an undo belongs to the conversation whose files it replaced. Maintenance evicts a conversation's refs by `damocles.checkpoints.retentionDays` or when its registered session file (`sessions/<sessionId>.json`) is gone and its newest ref is older than 24 hours; it never removes a folder repo as a whole and judges a conversation only by its own refs and registration.
- **gc runs off the folder lock, and still never drops what a ref or the index reaches.** Under the lock, maintenance evicts refs, rewrites `refs/damocles/index` (a commit of the shared index's tree, so blobs only the index references survive) and runs `pack-refs --all --prune`. It then runs `git repack -A -d -l` without the folder lock, under a separate `<repo>/repack` lock, and finally `git prune --expire=1.hour.ago` under the folder lock again. After a snapshot, when the repo has 50 or more packs, the producer starts `git repack -d -l --geometric=2` under the repack lock, off the folder lock and off its queue. Only `prune` deletes objects, and it runs under the folder lock, where no writer runs. Every writer (add, update-index, write-tree, commit-tree, hash-object) writes its objects and the refs or shared index that reach them within one lock hold, and git treats the shared index (`GIT_INDEX_FILE`) as a reachability root. `repack -A -d` without `--unpack-unreachable` never deletes: reachable objects go to the new pack and unreachable packed objects are written loose, and `--geometric` copies every object of the packs it rolls up. So an object a writer reuses during a repack is in the new pack or loose, and the next prune sees what reaches it. The one-hour prune grace protects only the lock-free rewind preview's throwaway objects.
- **Entries say which repo holds them.** A v3 `damocles-checkpoint` entry carries `repo: 'folder'`, `folderId` and `skipped`; new turns, including new turns in an old conversation, always write v3. A v2 entry (no `repo`) resolves to the conversation's legacy per-session repo through `getRepoDir(sessionFile)` exactly as before, for rewind, diff preview, file content and fork clone. Legacy repos are never migrated or widened; their existing age eviction retires them. A turn with no usable baseline gets a `{ v: 3, kind: 'not-rewindable', userEntryId, reason, params: { tool?, waitSeconds?, error? }, createdAt }` record under the same custom type; the webview renders its text from `reason` and `params`, and raw git text appears only as `params.error`. A pre-rewind snapshot gets a `{ v: 3, kind: 'pre-rewind', id, folderId, commit, skipped, target, createdAt }` record. `getCheckpointEntries` and `getCheckpointRecords` return neither, so every consumer of checkpoint entries still sees only entries with commits; the rewind view reads them through `getNotRewindableEntries` and `getPreRewindEntries`. Every validator checks every field and drops any other shape (an earlier unreleased v3 shape, or a v3 entry whose commits are not object ids) as no checkpoint; none throws. A restore or manifest read checks that the entry's `folderId` is cwd's and that the repo's `folder.json` names cwd, and otherwise touches nothing.
- **No pi handler awaits checkpoint git work.** pi awaits extension handlers before it forwards stream events and runs tools (`_handleAgentEvent`), and awaits `agent_settled` before `prompt()` resolves, so an awaited `git add -A` of a large folder held the first streamed text back for minutes. The `message_start`, `agent_settled` and `session_compact` handlers in `damocles-extension.ts` call synchronous `CheckpointService` methods that queue the work on the service's chain and return; there is no checkpoint `turn_end` handler. A turn's baseline starts at the prompt's own user entry, one microtask after its `message_end` (`watchPromptEntry`'s `onCommitted`): pi appends the message synchronously after notifying listeners, so the entry exists and no model request has gone out. A run opened without `sendMessage` (a cancel note, a re-steer) and a steered message start at the next assistant `message_start`, keyed on the branch's last user entry. A turn starts once per entry: the service remembers every entry it started and every entry a resumed or forked branch already has a record for, and the producer refuses a turn it already finalized, so a run keyed on a finished turn gets no second baseline and its gate waiters return at once. Finalize runs on the same chain after a still-running baseline and before the next turn's baseline, so it never delays the next prompt's stream. `checkpoint-stream-latency.test.ts` pins it on the real pi event path: another holder keeps the folder lock for the whole prompt, so the baseline cannot finish, and the first text must still stream and `prompt()` settle; it logs the time from the prompt entry's commit to the first text.
- **Records minted off the handler path are appended by the panel, and only to its live session.** The service hands each record, pre-rewind records included, to `PiSession.persistCheckpointRecord`, which appends it with `sessionManager.appendCustomEntry` only while the panel is not disposed, its runtime session still has that session id, and its lease was not lost. A delete, a switch, a lost lease and a panel close all move the runtime off the session and dispose the service first. A switch, a delete and a close first abort the running turn, then wait up to 2 s (`CHECKPOINT_DRAIN_MS`) for the queued checkpoint work while the session is still installed and the panel still registered. The abort resolves only after pi's `agent_settled`, whose handler queues the turn's finalize, so the drain covers the turn that was running, and no tool call runs after the teardown began or meets the unregistered gate's fallback block. A finalize that is nearly done still records the last turn; work still running after that (a first baseline of a large folder) is dropped, and its before-ref waits for age eviction. `dispose` stops the producer synchronously, so it writes no ref afterwards, and a late record is dropped with a log line. Never use `pi.appendEntry` from a continuation: the extension instance is shared by the folder's panels. A record lands at the leaf, which may be after the next prompt's user entry or between a tool call and its result. That is safe because a custom entry is not model context and every reader matches records by `userEntryId` or pre-rewind `id`, and tool results by `toolCallId`, never by position (`damocles-agent-invocation` entries already land mid-turn the same way).
- **File-changing tools wait for the turn's baseline in the permission gate, bounded by `damocles.checkpoints.baselineWaitSeconds`** (default 30, 5 to 600). `waitsForCheckpointBaseline` in `permission-gate.ts` decides, given the folder the checkpoint captures (`CheckpointBaselineGate.folder`, the session's cwd). Read tools, read-only-annotated MCP tools, shell commands `readonly-shell.ts` proves read-only (in every mode), the task and interactive tools, the in-process coordination tools (memory, Compass, browser, team messaging; none writes into the folder), and an Edit or Write whose target is the session plan file (`isPlanFilePath`) or outside the folder never wait and never mark the turn. The target resolves against the folder, so a path the check cannot place reads as inside and waits. Other Edit and Write, other shell (whatever path it names), MCP tools without a read-only annotation, unknown tools and the spawn tools (`Agent`, `create_team`, `resume_team`, `team_spawn_specialist`, `team_redispatch_specialist`) wait. The wait runs in the gate's single `proceed()`, after approval, so the approval prompt's time counts and a blocked call never waits. Configured PreToolUse hooks run before it, so a hook that itself writes into the folder is not bracketed by the turn's baseline; that is accepted. Past the limit the tool runs (a timeout is not a block, so no `formatPolicyBlockReason`), the turn is marked not rewindable (`markNotRewindable(userEntryId, 'baseline-timeout', { tool, waitSeconds })`), and the user gets one `notification` warning for the turn. The late baseline still finishes and warms the index, but it gets no ref and finalize yields the not-rewindable record, because the tool's changes may be in it. Later waiters in that turn return at once, and a later `message_start` for the same entry starts nothing. An abort, a dispose or a Stop of the waiting shell call releases a waiter at once. The wait does not register in `PermissionState`: the session is working, not waiting on the user. Each wait logs `[perf] checkpoint.wait <ms> outcome=<ready|timeout|aborted|disposed>`. The timer is the only bound; git is never killed on a timeout, which would leave `index.lock` in the shared repo.
- **Subagents and team agents wait on the parent turn's baseline through the same gate.** Their gate context carries the parent panel's `checkpointBaseline`, which reads the panel's live service at call time, so a nested file change never lands before the parent's current baseline, under the same limit, the same folder rule and the same single notice. A background agent still running after the parent's turn settled has no turn to wait for; its changes land in the next turn's window. This cannot deadlock: a baseline waits only on the service chain and the folder lock, no tool holds the lock, and every gate wait ends at the timer, an abort or a dispose.
- **Every restore first keeps the folder's current state, and the user can undo it.** A rewind to a turn and an undo both snapshot the folder exactly like a baseline (same excludes, protections and tracked capture) before anything changes, and log the commit (`pre-rewind snapshot <id> of session <sid> is commit <hash>`). If that snapshot fails, nothing is restored and the user is told no file changed. A v3 target is snapshotted and restored in one folder-lock hold; for a v2 target the snapshot goes to the folder repo of cwd under its lock, then the legacy restore takes the legacy repo's own lock, so no code nests two locks. The panel persists the pre-rewind record whenever there is one, including when the restore failed, and the rewind view lists each as a restore point, "Before rewind at 14:32" or "Before undo at 14:32", with an Undo rewind action (`undoRewind`). Undo (`restorePreRewind`) puts back the files of that snapshot through the same `safeCheckout` protections and takes its own pre-rewind snapshot, so an undo is undoable. It changes files only; the conversation tree is already navigable in pi. When the apply and its rollback both fail, the user's error says the folder may be partly restored and that the files from before the rewind are kept, and names both errors. The user's wait is bounded: `PiSession` gives a restore `RESTORE_WAIT_MS` (30 s) to get past earlier checkpoint work on the service chain and the folder lock; past it the restore resolves `aborted` at once and never runs later, and once it holds the lock it runs to completion. A missing or disposed service (`service-unavailable`) and a failed git probe (`git-unavailable`) are told apart. Every message is localized.
- **Files the project's own git tracks are always captured.** A snapshot captures every file the project's git tracks under the conversation folder, even under a path the exclude set drops (`dist/`, `build/`, `out/`, `coverage/`, `.cache/`, `node_modules/`, `*.log`, `.DS_Store`) or one the project's `.gitignore` matches (a force-added `.env.example`). The list comes from `git -C <cwd> -c core.fsmonitor=false ls-files -z`, which never writes the project's index or refs, cached per process while the project's index keeps its size and mtime. No enclosing repository means no tracked list, and a failed read is logged and read as empty. The security excludes (`.git`, `.damocles/settings.json`, `.damocles/settings.local.json`, `.damocles/mcp.local.json`) always win: a tracked copy is never captured or reported. The size cap, the category excludes and LFS still apply to tracked files, and each file they leave out is a skip in the checkpoint's list. Mechanism: `add -A`, then `update-index --add` of the tracked files the shared index lacks; once there, the stat cache keeps them current and `prepareSnapshot` keeps them, so an unchanged tracked file is never re-hashed. A submodule is tracked only as its gitlink, which is never staged, so files inside a submodule or a nested repository follow the exclude rules like untracked files; an embedded repository with no commit is left out with one log line instead of failing the folder. Only folder repos capture tracked files: a legacy per-session repo never does, in a restore or in the preview, so a tracked file a legacy repo excludes is ignored now and protected, neither deleted nor restored. For a folder-repo target, a tracked file the exclude rules drop is restored when the target has it and left where it is, never deleted, when the target lacks it (the target may predate the project tracking it, or the tracked list may have been unreadable then). When a directory now stands where the target has such a file, that path is protected, so nothing the directory holds is deleted. The rewind preview follows the same rules.
- **Large and binary files stay out of folder repos, and a rewind never touches them.** A folder repo's `info/exclude` holds the exclude set of its version (`FOLDER_CHECKPOINT_EXCLUDE_SET`: the performance excludes plus media, archives and disk images, model weights and array dumps, and Python, JVM and JS build and environment caches; images and PDFs are left to the size cap), every `filter=lfs` pattern of the project's `.gitattributes`, and a generated section listing every file over `damocles.checkpoints.maxFileSizeMB` (default 25, 1 to 2048). Before each snapshot the size section is rebuilt from lstat of the untracked and modified files plus the previous section, never reading contents, and files that are now ignored are dropped from the index unless the project tracks them, because an exclude never stops `add -A` from restaging a file already in the index. Every checkpoint stores its complete skipped list (path, bytes or null for a directory skipped whole, reason `size`, `category` or `lfs`) as a JSON blob in the folder repo, referenced by its `/skipped` ref so it lives exactly as long as the checkpoint, with no truncation. The session entry keeps only the summary: `{ totalCount, totalBytes, byReason: { size?, category?, lfs?: { count, bytes } }, patterns: [{ pattern, reason, count, bytes }], manifest }`. A skip counts under a category or LFS pattern only when `check-ignore -v` names the folder repo's own `info/exclude`; a project `.gitignore` line such as `*.mp4` is the project's own ignore, not a skip. The rewind view shows the summary under "Not restored by this checkpoint" and loads the full list when the user expands it (`requestSkippedFiles` answered by `skippedFiles`, read by `readSkippedManifest` without the folder lock, since a ref keeps the blob alive). A restore never modifies or deletes a file that is protected now: one ignored now (and not a tracked file a snapshot captures anyway), over the cap now, or recorded as skipped by the target snapshot. That includes a file captured small and grown past the cap since, which a rewind leaves as it is. A path that is ignored now but absent is recreated from the target, because that changes no existing bytes. The `clean -fd` after the apply protects every path ignored at the start of the restore by exact lines, so a restore that removes or replaces a `.gitignore` never deletes a file only that `.gitignore` protected. When a protected file or directory stands where the target needs a path, the restore refuses and touches nothing, because `read-tree -u` would delete an ignored file in its way. A turn's change list reports a file that grew past the cap during the turn as a skip, not a deletion.
